 ▝▝   ▝▝   E:\Projects\tm-game


❯ Extend my existing Godot 3D project with a physics-based object manipulation  
  system and a small physics testing playground.                                
  IMPORTANT: This project already contains a working FPS player controller,     
  camera and interaction system. It also has a voxel destruction add-on         
  installed.                                                                    
  Inspect the existing project first. Reuse and extend the current interaction  
  system rather than replacing it. Preserve existing movement, camera controls  
  and project configuration.                                                    
  1. Object manipulation                                                        
  Implement physics-based grabbing and carrying of objects using these          
  controls:                                                                     
                                                                                
  * E: Pick up the physics object you're looking at. Press E again to drop it.  
  * Hold middle mouse + move mouse: Rotate the held object freely in three      
  dimensions.                                                                   
  * Mouse wheel: Adjust the distance between the player and the held object.    
  * Left click: Throw the held object forwards.                                 
                                                                                
  While holding middle mouse, mouse movement must rotate the object instead of  
  rotating the camera.                                                          
  Objects should follow a position in front of the camera smoothly, using       
  physics-based movement rather than teleporting or being rigidly attached to   
  the camera.                                                                   
  Objects must retain appropriate collision behaviour while held. They should   
  not pass through walls, floors or other solid objects.                        
  Allow the player to walk and sprint normally while carrying objects.          
  Prevent held objects from violently shaking, spinning uncontrollably or       
  pushing the player around.                                                    
  Respect object mass where practical, allowing lighter objects to be           
  manipulated more easily than heavy ones.                                      
  2. Physics test objects                                                       
  Create several reusable objects with visibly different physical properties:   
                                                                                
  * Wooden crate: A standard rigid-body box that can be picked up, stacked,     
  rotated and thrown.                                                           
  * Rubber ball: A sphere with high restitution that bounces convincingly when  
  dropped or thrown.                                                            
  * Heavy metal box: A heavier object that is harder to move and produces more  
  substantial impacts.                                                          
  * Lightweight cardboard box: A light object that is easy to carry and throw.  
  * Cylinder: A physics object that can roll, tip over and be balanced.         
                                                                                
  Use straightforward, recognisable materials and visually distinguish the      
  objects.                                                                      
  Configure their mass, friction, damping and bounce properties appropriately.  
  3. Physics playground                                                         
  Create a simple test environment containing:                                  
                                                                                
  * A flat floor.                                                               
  * Several physics objects.                                                    
  * A sturdy table for stacking and placing objects.                            
  * A raised platform for dropping objects from a height.                       
  * A wall for throwing objects against.                                        
  * Enough open space to experiment with object manipulation.                   
                                                                                
  The environment should remain simple. This is a physics and interaction       
  prototype, not a full game level.                                             
  4. Rotation and placement                                                     
  Ensure objects can be positioned precisely.                                   
                                                                                
  * Support rotation around all three axes.                                     
  * Allow fine adjustments while holding an object.                             
  * Preserve the object's orientation when dropped.                             
  * Allow objects to rest naturally on tables, floors and other objects.        
  * Support stacking boxes and balancing objects.                               
  * Avoid automatically snapping objects to an artificial grid.                 
                                                                                
  5. Technical requirements                                                     
  Use Godot's native 3D physics systems, including RigidBody3D,                 
  CollisionShape3D and PhysicsMaterial where appropriate.                       
  Keep the manipulation system reusable so it can later work with furniture,    
  tools, destructible voxel objects and construction materials.                 
  Do not implement voxel destruction or construction yet. Leave the installed   
  add-on intact.                                                                
  Avoid changing unrelated systems.                                             
  6. Testing                                                                    
  Test the actual project and verify that:                                      
                                                                                
  * Objects can be picked up and dropped.                                       
  * Mouse rotation works across all three axes.                                 
  * Scrolling adjusts holding distance.                                         
  * Throwing works correctly.                                                   
  * The rubber ball bounces.                                                    
  * Heavy and light objects behave differently.                                 
  * Objects collide correctly while held.                                       
  * Boxes can be stacked.                                                       
  * Existing FPS controls still work.                                           
  * The project loads without errors.                                           
                                                                                
  Goal: A fully playable first-person physics playground that establishes a     
  reliable, satisfying object manipulation system for a future                  
  Taskmaster-inspired immersive sim.                                            
  Prioritise responsive controls, believable physics, stability and reusable    
  architecture over additional features.

      190 +func _push_rigid_bodies(intended_velocity: Vector3, delta: flo
          +at) -> void:                                                  
      191 +  for index in get_slide_collision_count():                   
      192 +    var hit := get_slide_collision(index)                     
      193 +    var body := hit.get_collider() as RigidBody3D             
      194 +    # Standing on a body should not shove it along.           
      195 +    if body == null or body.freeze or hit.get_normal().dot(up_
          +direction) > 0.7:                                             
      196 +      continue                                                
      197 +    var push := -hit.get_normal().slide(up_direction).normaliz
          +ed()                                                          
      198 +    # Only push until the body keeps pace, so light objects ar
          +e not launched.                                               
      199 +    if push.is_zero_approx() or body.linear_velocity.dot(push)
          + >= intended_velocity.dot(push):                              
      200 +      continue                                                
      201 +    body.apply_impulse(push * push_force * delta, hit.get_posi
          +tion() - body.global_position)

● Since a child's _ready runs before the parent's, the player's camera/head
  won't be set yet—so I'll wait for the holder to be ready and skip the grab if
  the camera still isn't available.

● I've fixed the crash. Pressing E now picks objects up instead of throwing the
  Nil error.

  Cause: I hand-wrote the ObjectManipulator node in player.tscn without the
  node_paths=PackedStringArray("view", "camera") line. Godot 4 needs that line
  for exported node references saved as paths. Without it, camera and view
  stayed null, and the first grab crashed when it tried to read the camera
  position.

  Fix:
  - player.tscn: added the missing node_paths line.
  - object_manipulator.gd: if camera or view aren't assigned, it now falls back
    to the player's own camera and head. If there's still no camera, it logs a
    clear error and refuses to grab instead of crashing.

  Testing: I wrote a scripted test that loads the playground and runs the
  manipulator with real physics. All 31 checks pass:
  - Grab and drop work. The object follows the hold point, and its orientation
    is unchanged at the moment of drop.
  - Rotation works on all three axes: mouse X turns it, mouse Y tips it forward
    or back, and middle mouse + wheel rolls it. The camera doesn't move while
    you rotate.
  - The scroll wheel changes holding distance.
  - A held crate pushed into the wall never got inside it, never went into the
    floor, and didn't push the player.
  - Throw speed depends on mass: 16 m/s for cardboard, 2 m/s for the metal box.
    The heavy box also takes about twice as long to catch up when lifted.
  - The rubber ball rebounds to 2 m when dropped from 3 m. A dropped crate
    barely bounces.
  - The pre-placed crate stack stays standing, and a crate carried over and
    dropped onto it rests on top.
  - Both the playground and your original main scene load with no runtime
    errors.

  Four checks failed on early runs, all from how I'd set up the test. I'd
  rotated a crate that was still resting on the table, and teleported the player
  so a crate flew straight through the stack. I fixed the setup rather than

● All five objects are now driven by .tres files. You can tune them in the
  inspector, and a new object is just a new .tres.

  How it's set up:
  - physics_object_definition.gd is the resource script. Its inspector is
    grouped:
    - Identity: name, grabbable.
    - Shape: box, sphere, cylinder or capsule. Only the size fields that shape
      uses are shown. There's also an optional custom collision shape.
    - Physics: mass, friction, bounce, rough, absorbent, damping, continuous
      collision.
    - Collision: layers and mask, as checkboxes.
    - Visuals: a material you edit inline, an optional custom mesh, and an
      optional detail scene.
  - physics_prop.gd is the node, and it builds itself from a definition. It runs
    in the editor, so props preview in the viewport. Editing a .tres updates
    every prop that uses it, live. The built collision and mesh aren't saved
    into your scenes, so the .tres stays the only place a value lives.
  - props/*.tres are the five objects, with the same values as before.
  - props/details/ holds the decorative trim: crate frame, tape, metal bands,
    stripes. One caveat: these keep their size when you resize a shape, so if
    you change a crate's size, its frame won't follow.
  - Playground: the props are now PhysicsProp nodes using the .tres files. Names
    and positions are unchanged, and the old per-object .tscn files are gone.

  To add a new object: in the FileSystem dock, right-click → New Resource →
  PhysicsObjectDefinition, fill it in, and save it as a .tres. Then add a
  PhysicsProp node (search for it in Add Node) and drag the .tres onto its
  Definition slot. From code it's one line:
  PhysicsProp.create(load("res://props/my_thing.tres")).

  Testing: I re-ran the full behaviour test. Grabbing, all three rotation axes,
  scroll distance, throwing, the heavy vs light difference, wall and floor
  collision and stacking all pass as before. New checks also pass: props take
  their values from the .tres, a definition made purely in code works (I tried a
  capsule), and editing a definition rebuilds every prop using it. Both scenes
  load with no errors.

  The .tres files load in your open editor too, since it re-saved them. I
  haven't clicked through the inspector myself, though, so tell me if anything
  there looks off.

❯ Godot Physics Sandbox — Physical Inventory, Containers and Equippable Tools   
  Extend my existing Godot 4 first-person physics sandbox with a physical       
  inventory, functional containers and an equippable tool system.               
  This is the next development stage of a Taskmaster-inspired immersive         
  simulation with physics, destruction and construction.                        
  Inspect the existing project before making changes. It already contains:      
                                                                                
  * A working FPS controller and camera.                                        
  * An E-based interaction system.                                              
  * Physics-based object grabbing, carrying, rotation, distance adjustment,     
  dropping and throwing.                                                        
  * Multiple physics props with different masses and physical properties.       
  * A physics playground.                                                       
  * A data-driven physics object system using custom Resources (`.tres`).       
  * An installed voxel destruction add-on.                                      
                                                                                
  Build on these existing systems. Preserve all working functionality and avoid 
  unnecessary rewrites.                                                         
  1. Physical inventory — no traditional inventory                              
  The player should interact with physical objects directly rather than storing 
  everything in an invisible inventory.                                         
  Distinguish between three states:                                             
                                                                                
  * World object: An ordinary physics object lying in the environment.          
  * Held object: An object being carried and positioned using the existing      
  physics manipulation system.                                                  
  * Equipped tool: A tool held in a dedicated first-person position, ready to   
  be used.                                                                      
                                                                                
  The player can hold one object or equip one tool at a time, using a           
  consistent, clearly defined hand/holding system.                              
  Objects should transition correctly between these states without duplication, 
  deletion or loss of their individual data.                                    
  Do not add an inventory grid, hotbar, backpack or unlimited item storage.     
  2. Physical containers                                                        
  Create a reusable container system that allows physics objects to be placed   
  inside other objects.                                                         
  Start with:                                                                   
                                                                                
  * An open wooden box.                                                         
  * A plastic basket or storage crate.                                          
  * A bucket.                                                                   
                                                                                
  Containers should be ordinary physical objects that the player can pick up,   
  carry, rotate, drop and throw using the existing manipulation system.         
  Container behaviour                                                           
                                                                                
  * Allow players to place multiple smaller physics objects inside a container. 
  * Contents must occupy actual physical space.                                 
  * Objects should collide with the container walls and floor.                  
  * Objects must remain inside when the container is lifted or transported      
  normally.                                                                     
  * Tilting or overturning the container should allow objects to slide or fall  
  out.                                                                          
  * Throwing a container should allow its contents to spill naturally.          
  * Objects can be individually retrieved from inside a container.              
  * Container contents should contribute to its total carried weight and        
  physical behaviour.                                                           
  * Prevent objects from clipping through container walls or falling through    
  the bottom.                                                                   
                                                                                
  Use actual physics wherever practical, with carefully chosen stabilisation or 
  collision handling if needed.                                                 
  Do not fake the system by simply storing item IDs in a container inventory.   
  Reusability                                                                   
  The container system should be sufficiently general to support future objects 
  such as:                                                                      
                                                                                
  * Wheelbarrows.                                                               
  * Bins.                                                                       
  * Trolleys.                                                                   
  * Trays.                                                                      
  * Drawers.                                                                    
  * Toolboxes.                                                                  
  * Buckets and other vessels.                                                  
                                                                                
  Do not implement all of these yet. Establish a reusable foundation that can   
  accommodate them later.                                                       
  3. Equippable tools                                                           
  Extend the existing physics object system to support equippable tools.        
  A tool should exist as an ordinary physical object in the world until         
  equipped.                                                                     
  For example, the player should be able to:                                    
                                                                                
  1. Look at a hammer.                                                          
  2. Press E to pick it up using the existing physics manipulation system.      
  3. Press a separate key to equip it.                                          
  4. See the hammer move into a natural first-person holding position.          
  5. Use the hammer with the mouse.                                             
  6. Unequip it, returning it to normal physical manipulation.                  
  7. Drop it back into the world or place it inside a container.                
                                                                                
  Use F as the default Equip/Unequip key, unless F is already assigned to an    
  existing function. If it is, inspect the input mappings and choose an         
  available key.                                                                
  Preserve E for grabbing and dropping objects.                                 
  Equipped tool behaviour                                                       
                                                                                
  * Only one tool may be equipped at a time.                                    
  * The equipped object should be visually attached to the player's hand or     
  appropriate first-person holding position.                                    
  * Preserve the identity and properties of the original physics object.        
  * Support different tool sizes, orientations and grip positions.              
  * Prevent equipped tools from obstructing the camera excessively.             
  * Avoid unwanted physics collisions between the equipped tool and the player. 
  * Unequipping or dropping should return the original object to normal world   
  physics.                                                                      
  * Do not create duplicate objects during equipping.                           
                                                                                
  Keep tool configuration data-driven through the existing Resource-based       
  object system.                                                                
  4. Tool usage and animations                                                  
  Implement a reusable tool-action system.                                      
  When a tool is equipped:                                                      
                                                                                
  * Left click activates its primary action.                                    
  * Tools should have appropriate first-person use animations.                  
  * The action should originate from the actual equipped tool.                  
  * Use collision detection, raycasts or appropriate physics queries to detect  
  impacts.                                                                      
  * Impacts should interact with ordinary physics objects.                      
  * Tool use must respect reasonable timing and cooldowns.                      
  * Prevent rapid animation or input glitches.                                  
                                                                                
  Start with three tools.                                                       
  Hammer                                                                        
                                                                                
  * Equip and hold naturally.                                                   
  * Left click swings the hammer.                                               
  * Detect impacts against physical objects.                                    
  * Apply a suitable physical impulse.                                          
  * Provide simple impact feedback.                                             
                                                                                
  Golf club                                                                     
                                                                                
  * Equip with an appropriate grip.                                             
  * Left click swings the club.                                                 
  * Strike physics objects, especially balls.                                   
  * Transfer an appropriate impulse based on the swing direction.               
  * Make it possible to aim and hit a ball across the playground.               
                                                                                
  Basic handheld tool                                                           
  Add a third simple tool, such as a wooden bat or similar striking implement,  
  to demonstrate that the action system supports multiple tools without bespoke 
  player-controller code.                                                       
  Tools should behave differently through configurable properties such as       
  force, range, swing speed, mass and impact type.                              
  Do not implement full voxel destruction yet. Establish the tool interaction   
  and impact framework so the voxel destruction system can be integrated later. 
  5. Data-driven integration                                                    
  Extend the existing `.tres` resource architecture instead of creating an      
  unrelated inventory or tool definition system.                                
  Allow relevant properties to be configured in the Godot Inspector, such as:   
                                                                                
  * Whether an object is equippable.                                            
  * Tool category and primary action.                                           
  * Equipped position and rotation offsets.                                     
  * Swing speed and impact strength.                                            
  * Interaction range.                                                          
  * Container eligibility and physical characteristics.                         
  * Appropriate collision behaviour.                                            
                                                                                
  Keep object definitions separate from individual runtime object states.       
  Multiple instances using the same Resource must still behave independently.   
  Avoid hardcoding individual object names into the player controller.          
  6. Physics playground additions                                               
  Expand the existing playground with a dedicated testing area.                 
  Include:                                                                      
                                                                                
  * Several empty containers.                                                   
  * Multiple loose wooden blocks and other small objects.                       
  * A few balls.                                                                
  * A hammer.                                                                   
  * A golf club.                                                                
  * A third basic striking tool.                                                
  * A table or workbench for manipulating tools and objects.                    
  * Space for carrying loaded containers and tipping them over.                 
                                                                                
  Reuse the existing physics props and scene wherever practical.                
  Keep the playground simple and functional.                                    
  7. Input controls                                                             
  Preserve the existing controls:                                               
                                                                                
  * WASD — movement.                                                            
  * Existing sprint and jump controls.                                          
  * E — pick up/drop physics objects.                                           
  * Middle mouse + mouse movement — rotate held objects.                        
  * Mouse wheel — adjust holding distance.                                      
  * Left click — throw an ordinary held object.                                 
                                                                                
  Add:                                                                          
                                                                                
  * F — equip/unequip a held tool, provided the key is available.               
  * Left click while a tool is equipped — use its primary action.               
                                                                                
  Ensure throwing and tool use cannot accidentally trigger simultaneously.      
  Preserve the camera behaviour when rotating ordinary held objects.            
  Do not break existing controls.                                               
  8. Testing and acceptance                                                     
  Test the actual Godot project and check the following:                        
                                                                                
  * Pick up a wooden block and put it into an open crate.                       
  * Add several objects to the same crate.                                      
  * Carry the loaded crate around the playground.                               
  * Drop the crate and verify its contents remain physical.                     
  * Tip the crate over and watch the objects spill out.                         
  * Pick an individual object out of the crate.                                 
  * Put a hammer into the crate and retrieve it.                                
  * Equip the hammer and swing it at a physics object.                          
  * Unequip the hammer and return it to the crate.                              
  * Equip a golf club and strike a bouncing ball.                               
  * Confirm multiple instances of the same resource remain independent.         
  * Confirm the player can still use all previously implemented grabbing,       
  carrying, rotation and throwing controls.                                     
  * Confirm there are no unwanted object duplicates, lost objects, collision    
  failures or runtime errors.                                                   
                                                                                
  Where automated tests cannot exercise real mouse input, explain that          
  limitation and verify the underlying systems separately.                      
  Do not claim manual controls have been tested if they have not.               
  Final goal                                                                    
  Create a genuinely physical object-handling environment in which the player   
  can collect, transport, spill, retrieve, equip and use objects without        
  relying on a traditional inventory.                                           
  The entire system should feel natural, consistent and reusable.               
  This will form the foundation for future voxel destruction, construction,     
  improvised tools, wheelbarrows, movable furniture and open-ended              
  Taskmaster-style challenges.                                                  
  Prioritise working physics, responsive controls, stable interactions and      
  reusable architecture. Preserve everything that already works.

      276 +## Hits what the crosshair is on: an exact ray first, then a w
          +ider sphere as aim assist.                                    
      277 +func _crosshair_hit(impact_time: float) -> void:              
      278 +  _hit_done = true                                            
      279 +  var aim := _crosshair_transform()                           
      280 +  var forward := -aim.basis.z.normalized()                    
      281 +  var space := _tool.get_world_3d().direct_space_state        
      282 +  var exclude: Array[RID] = [_holder.get_rid(), _tool.get_rid(
          +)]                                                            
      283 +  var ray := PhysicsRayQueryParameters3D.create(aim.origin, ai
          +m.origin + forward * _settings.reach, _settings.hit_mask, excl
          +ude)                                                          
      284 +  var hit := space.intersect_ray(ray)                         
      285 +  var point: Vector3                                          
      286 +  var normal: Vector3                                         
      287 +  if not hit.is_empty():                                      
      288 +    point = hit["position"]                                   
      289 +    normal = hit["normal"]                                    
      290 +  elif _settings.aim_assist_radius > 0.0:                     
      291 +    _sphere.radius = _settings.aim_assist_radius              
      292 +    _query.shape = _sphere                                    
      293 +    _query.collision_mask = _settings.hit_mask                
      294 +    _query.exclude = exclude                                  
      295 +    _query.transform = Transform3D(Basis(), aim.origin)       
      296 +    _query.motion = forward * (_settings.reach - _settings.aim
          +_assist_radius)                                               
      297 +    var fractions := space.cast_motion(_query)                
      298 +    if fractions.is_empty() or fractions[1] >= 1.0:           
      299 +      return                                                  
      300 +    _query.transform = Transform3D(Basis(), aim.origin + _quer
          +y.motion * fractions[1])                                      
      301 +    _query.motion = Vector3.ZERO                              
      302 +    hit = space.get_rest_info(_query)                         
      303 +    if hit.is_empty():                                        
      304 +      return                                                  
      305 +    hit["collider"] = instance_from_id(hit["collider_id"])    
      306 +    point = hit["point"]                                      
      307 +    normal = hit["normal"]                                    
      308 +  else:                                                       
      309 +    return                                                    
      310 +  var collider: Object = hit["collider"]                      
      311 +  if collider == null:                                        
      312 +    return                                                    
      313 +  # Report the swing's own direction and speed, so impacts sti
          +ll feel like the tool's.                                      
      314 +  var step := 1.0 / Engine.physics_ticks_per_second           
      315 +  var path := _strike_point_at(impact_time) - _strike_point_at
          +(impact_time - step)                                          
      316 +  _register_hit(collider, point, normal, path.normalized() if 
          +not path.is_zero_approx() else forward, path.length() / step) 
      317 +                                                              
      318 +                                                              
      319 +## Where the crosshair points from: the camera when the holder
          + has one, else the head.                                      
      320 +func _crosshair_transform() -> Transform3D:                   
      321 +  if manipulator != null and manipulator.camera != null:      
      322 +    return manipulator.camera.global_transform                
      323 +  return view.global_transform                                
      324 +                                                              
      325 +                                                              
      326  func _sweep(from_time: float, to_time: float) -> void:
      327    var from := _strike_point_at(from_time)
      328    var to := _strike_point_at(to_time)

❯ This is awesome. Thank you very much. Um, next up is chargeable throws and

● Here's the plan, which builds on the existing throw and swing code:

  - Hold LMB to charge, release to fire. A quick tap gives a minimum-power throw
    or swing. Holding builds up to full power over a charge time.
  - Everything is configurable:
    - Throws, on the ObjectManipulator node: charge time, tap power and max
      power.
    - Tools, per tool in each tool's .tres: charge time, min power and max
      power. Setting max power above 1 makes a full charge stronger than the
      current swing.
  - Power scales everything: the impulse, the speed cap for light objects, and
    the damage reported on impacts.
  - Feedback: a small charge bar under the crosshair that turns orange at full.
    Tools hold their windup pose while charging, and held objects draw back
    slightly toward you.

  Let me look at the current state of the two scripts first:

  Charged throws and strikes:
  - Hold LMB to charge and release to fire. A quick tap is weak; holding builds
    to full power. A bar under the crosshair fills and turns orange at full.
  - While charging, a held object draws back toward you a little, and a tool
    holds its wind-up pose. Pressing again mid-swing is ignored.
  - Power scales everything: throw speed, tool impulse, the speed cap for light
    objects, and the damage reported on impacts.
  - Throw settings are on the player's ObjectManipulator node: Throw Charge Time
    (0.8 s), Min Throw Power (0.3) and Max Throw Power (1.0).
  - Tool settings are under a Charging group in each tool's .tres: Charge Time
    (0.6 s), Min Charge Power (0.3) and Max Charge Power (1.0).
  - Set a max power above 1 if you want a full charge to hit harder than before.
    Set a charge time of 0 to make that throw or tool fire instantly.

    swing.

  - Releasing swings it down through the ball and follows through to the left.
  - The ball still flies where you're facing, and the hit still lands where the
    crosshair is. To aim, look down at the ball.

    in the inspector. The Rest/Windup/Strike Position Y values (about −0.7)

❯ Destruction — the first proper experiment                                     
  Start with a simple voxel wall in your existing playground.                   
  You equip your hammer, charge a swing, and strike it. Instead of merely       
  pushing the wall, you physically remove voxels around the impact point.       
  The important details are that the wall develops actual holes, its collision  
  updates, and different impact strengths cause different amounts of damage.    
  Use the voxel destruction add-on you've already installed, provided it        
  supports those capabilities. Opus should inspect it before deciding how to    
  integrate it.                                                                 
  2. Structural destruction                                                     
  This is where things get properly interesting.                                
  Imagine destroying the bottom of a voxel pillar. Eventually, the unsupported  
  upper section should detach and fall.                                         
  Those detached pieces become physical objects that can interact with your     
  existing grabbing and throwing system.                                        
  That's a much more complicated step, so I'd establish basic voxel removal     
  before tackling structural collapse.                                          
  3. Construction                                                               
  And then we reverse the process.                                              
  Give the player a way to place voxels, repair holes and build simple          
  structures using the same voxel system.                                       
  Build a wall. Smash it with a hammer. Pick up the debris. Rebuild it. /plan

  ⎿  Plan saved to: C:\Users\evans\.claude\plans\piped-weaving-lark.md · /plan 
     to edit
     Voxel destruction, structural collapse and construction

     Context

     Next stage of the Taskmaster-style physics sandbox: build a wall, smash it
     with the (charged) hammer, watch unsupported parts collapse, pick up the
     debris, and rebuild. The project already has the hooks for this: tools
     report impacts through receive_tool_impact(impact: ToolImpact) with damage
     and power (charge-scaled), the grab/throw system works with any RigidBody3D
     that exposes the prop contract (can_be_grabbed, get_grab_name,
     get_carried_mass, on_grabbed/on_released), and everything is configured
     through .tres definitions.

     Add-on assessment (decides the integration)

     addons/VoxelDestruction (v1.2) was inspected:
     - Holes and collision updates: supported. VoxelDamager.hit() (an Area3D)
       gives per-voxel health and greedy box collision rebuilds per chunk.
     - Structural collapse: not reliable. "Flood fill" is marked experimental,
       and it anchors the largest group rather than the ground, so breaking a
       pillar's base can drop the base instead of the top. Detached pieces get
       one AABB collider, are auto-deleted after debris_lifetime, and aren't
       grabbable. The "Voxel Object" mode has a TODO for broken mesh generation
       (voxel_object.gd:1027–1154).
     - Construction: impossible. There is no API to add voxels. The voxel set
       and multimesh are fixed when a .vox is imported and populated in the
       editor; voxels can only be hidden.
     - It is async/threaded and has an optional Rust extension, so phases 2–3
       would need a deep rewrite of third-party code.

     Decision: build a compact voxel system in project code, designed for all
     three phases. Leave the add-on installed and untouched. Its greedy-box
     algorithm (voxel_object.gd:731 _create_shapes) is the reference for our
     collision merger.

     User choices: construction = both (a free-placement trowel and cementing
     debris back in). Rubble = some knocked-out voxels become grabbable props
     (capped); the rest become particles.

     Architecture (new folder physics/voxels/)

     File: voxel_material_definition.gd (Resource)
     Role: name, colour, colour jitter, strength (health per voxel), density
     (kg/m³), friction, bounce, rubble_fraction. Example .tres: brick, concrete,

     plaster.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     File: voxel_structure_definition.gd (Resource)
     Role: Authored shapes: voxel_size (default 0.1 m), list of filled boxes
     (cell
     ranges + material), anchor_mode (BOTTOM_LAYER / NONE).
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     File: voxel_body.gd (class_name VoxelBody extends RigidBody3D)
     Role: One class for everything. Frozen (static freeze mode) for anchored
     structures; unfrozen for detached chunks and rubble. Sparse data:
     Dictionary[Vector3i, int] cell → palette index, plus health dict, anchor
     set
     and palette Array[VoxelMaterialDefinition]. Mesh and collision are rebuilt
     per 16³ chunk, only for dirty chunks, batched once per physics frame.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     File: voxel_mesher.gd (static)
     Role: Face-culled chunk mesh via SurfaceTool. Vertex colours carry material

     colour, per-voxel jitter and darkening by damage. One shared vertex-colour
     material.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     File: voxel_collider.gd (static)
     Role: Greedy box merge per chunk → BoxShape3D CollisionShape3Ds (internal
     children, like PhysicsProp).
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     File: voxel_connectivity.gd (static)
     Role: BFS components; finds what is still connected to anchors.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     File: voxel_construction.gd (static)
     Role: Trowel placement target/commit and "cement held chunk into
     structure".

     VoxelBody implements the existing prop contract, so detached chunks and
     rubble work with ObjectManipulator, containers (PhysicsContainer tracks any
     RigidBody3D on layer 16) and throwing, with no changes to those systems.
     Mass = voxels × voxel volume × density; big chunks are naturally "too
     heavy" (max_carry_mass 80 kg). Layer 16 (props) with mask 31 matches
     existing props, so the interaction ray and hammer hit_mask already reach
     it. It joins physics_props: on R reset, authored structures rebuild from
     their definition, and spawned chunks and rubble are freed.

     Phase 1: Voxel removal (stop for user play-test after)

     - VoxelBody.receive_tool_impact(impact): hit point nudged inward by -normal
       × voxel_size/4 → cell. Every cell within radius r = 
       tool.voxel_damage_radius × sqrt(impact.power) takes impact.damage × (1 − 
       d/r). A voxel is removed when health ≤ 0, and partially damaged voxels
       darken. Charge power already scales damage (tool_handler.gd 
       _register_hit), so a tap only cracks and a full swing punches a hole.
     - On a frozen structure, set impact.handled = true (no impulse). Chunks
       still get the normal impulse.
     - Add a voxel_damage_radius field to PhysicsToolDefinition (Impact group).
       Values: hammer 0.25 m, bat 0.2 m, golf club 0.08 m, so tools differ
       through data only.
     - Feedback: give ImpactFeedback.spawn an optional colour argument for
       material-coloured particle bursts.
     - Rubble: each removed voxel has a rubble_fraction chance to spawn as a
       1-voxel dynamic VoxelBody flung along the impact direction. A static
       registry caps live rubble (~60); the oldest shrink and free. The rest
       become particles.
     - Collision rebuilds in the same frame, so you can walk or see through
       holes and throw objects through them.

     Phase 2: Structural collapse (stop for play-test)

     - After removals on a body, run connectivity once (batched per frame). On a
       frozen structure, components with no anchor cell detach. On a dynamic
       body, the largest component stays and the others split off.
     - Each detached component becomes a new unfrozen VoxelBody, re-centred by a
       whole-cell offset (keeps the grid aligned, and the origin near the mass
       so the manipulator holds it sensibly). It copies palette and health and
       inherits the parent's point velocity.
     - Anchors: authored structures use BOTTOM_LAYER. Built cells become anchors
       when placed directly on non-voxel static geometry (a short downward ray
       test).
     - Detached pieces persist as real objects: grabbable if light enough,
       throwable, hammerable (they break further), and they can go in
       containers.

     Phase 3: Construction (stop for play-test)

     - Trowel tool (props/trowel.tres, compound handle + blade). Add
       PrimaryAction.PLACE_VOXEL with place_material and place_voxel_size. Uses
       the existing crosshair ray and reach, no charge; holding LMB repeats
       every cooldown. A translucent ghost cube previews the target cell while
       equipped.
       - Aiming at a VoxelBody places into the adjacent cell (hit point + normal
         × half a voxel), which also repairs holes.
       - Aiming at the floor or a wall starts a new frozen structure on a
         world-aligned grid, anchored if resting on static geometry.
       - Placement is blocked if the cell overlaps the player.
     - Cement: while holding a VoxelBody chunk or rubble near a frozen
       structure, press C (new input action cement_held; C is unbound). The
       chunk's rotation snaps to the nearest 90° alignment with the structure.
       Each chunk voxel maps to its nearest structure cell, and empty cells
       connected to the structure are filled with the chunk's material and
       health. Voxels that can't place stay behind as a smaller chunk, so
       material is conserved and nothing is duplicated. The prompt shows [C] 
       Cement when a structure is within ~1.5 voxels.
     - Integration: ToolHandler delegates PLACE_VOXEL to VoxelConstruction,
       keeping the swing timeline for a short poke animation. The player's hold
       branch adds the C handling and hint next to the existing F hint
       (character_body_3d.gd _update_interaction).

     Playground

     New scene playground/voxel_test_area.tscn, instanced into the playground
     with one line. It goes in a separate file because the open editor
     overwrites the playground. It sits in free space north-east of the tool
     bench (≈ x 11–17, z −9 to −4) and contains:
     - a brick wall (2.4 × 1.8 × 0.2 m, 0.1 m voxels ≈ 8.6k voxels);
     - a pillar topped with an overhanging slab (for collapse);
     - a small two-legged bridge;
     - a table with the trowel and a few loose brick chunks.

     Files touched

     - New: physics/voxels/*.gd (6), props/voxel_materials/*.tres,
       props/voxel_structures/*.tres, props/trowel.tres,
       playground/voxel_test_area.tscn.
     - Edited (small):
       - physics/physics_tool_definition.gd: voxel_damage_radius, PLACE_VOXEL
         and place fields.
       - physics/tool_handler.gd: place-action branch and ghost preview.
       - physics/impact_feedback.gd: colour argument.
       - character_body_3d.gd: C cement key and prompt.
       - project.godot: cement_held action.
       - Hammer, bat and club .tres: radius values.
       - playground/physics_playground.tscn: one instance line.
     - Untouched: addons/VoxelDestruction, the movement/camera code, manipulator
       internals and containers.

     Verification

     Per your preference I won't write automated scenario tests. After each
     phase I'll run one headless load of the playground to catch parse and
     runtime errors, then hand over a short play-test checklist:
     - P1: a tap cracks/darkens while a full charge punches a hole; you can see
       and throw through the hole; rubble pieces can be picked up; R restores
       the wall.
     - P2: knocking out the pillar's base drops the slab as a physical piece;
       breaking a bridge leg drops the span; light pieces can be grabbed and
       thrown and fit in a crate; hammering a chunk splits it.
     - P3: the trowel ghost tracks the crosshair; you can build a small wall on
       the floor and fill a hole; you can carry a broken chunk back, press C to
       cement it in, and the leftover voxels stay as a chunk.

● Starting phase 1 (voxel removal) designed to integrate cleanly with phases 2
  and 3, running only a quick load check at the end since you'll handle testing.
  First I'll set up the data resources: material, fill box, and structure.

● Phase 1 (voxel removal) is built and the playground loads without errors. I
  haven't play-tested it.

  Where to find it: north-east of the tool workbench there's a brick wall, a
  brick pillar with a concrete slab on top, and a small bridge. Grab the hammer
  from the bench and go at them.

  How it works:
  - Holes: a strike damages every voxel within a radius of the hit point, with
    the most damage at the centre. Voxels that run out of health are removed.
    The mesh and collision of just the affected section rebuild that frame.
  - Charge matters: a tap does 7.5 damage, which only cracks brick (damaged
    voxels darken), and repeated taps on one spot wear through. A full charge
    does 25 damage over a larger radius and punches a hole about 30 cm across.
  - Materials: concrete needs roughly twice the damage of brick. Each tool now
    has a Voxel Damage Radius setting (hammer 0.25 m, bat 0.2 m, golf club 0.08
    m). The golf club barely scratches the wall.
  - Rubble: about 20% of knocked-out voxels fly off as small grabbable pieces;
    the rest become dust. Live rubble is capped at 60, and the oldest shrinks
    away.
  - R restores the structures.

  Not in yet:
  - Collapse is phase 2. If you cut through the pillar now, the top will float.
  - The trowel and cementing are phase 3.

  Things to try:
  1. Tap the wall a few times in one spot, then charge a full swing somewhere
     else, and compare.
  2. Smash through until you can see through the hole, then throw a ball through
     it.
  3. Pick up some rubble and drop it in a crate.
  4. Press R and check the wall comes back.

  I added the voxel area to the playground with one instance line, and your
  editor may still hold an old copy of the playground. If it asks to reload the
  scene from disk, say yes. If you save the playground without reloading, the
  voxel area will disappear; it lives in playground/voxel_test_area.tscn if you
  need to re-add it.

  Once you've had a go and you're happy, I'll start phase 2 (structural
  collapse).

● Quick load check:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Phase 2, structural collapse, is in. The playground loads without errors; I
  haven't play-tested it.

  How it works:
  - Whenever voxels are removed, the body checks which voxels are still
    connected to each other, face to face. That check runs once per frame,
    before the meshes rebuild.
  - Walls, pillars and bridges are anchored by their bottom layer. Any group of
    voxels that's no longer connected to an anchor breaks off as a new free
    physics piece and falls. If you somehow destroy every anchor, the whole
    structure comes loose.
  - Loose pieces keep their largest connected part when hit, and anything else
    splits off. You can keep breaking a fallen chunk into smaller ones.
  - New pieces are real objects.
    - They keep their materials and any damage already done.
    - Their weight comes from their voxels: brick is 1.9 kg per voxel, concrete
      2.4 kg.
    - Pieces up to 80 kg can be picked up, rotated, thrown and put in
      containers. Heavier ones show "too heavy" when you look at them.
    - A breakaway of one or two voxels counts as rubble, under the same 60-piece
      cap.
  - R clears all broken pieces and rebuilds the original structures.

  Things to try:
  1. Chip through the bottom of the pillar on all sides. The upper pillar and
     the slab should come down together as one piece.
  2. Knock out one bridge leg. The deck should stay up on the other leg. Then
     cut the deck near that leg and the span drops.
  3. Cut a notch across the wall until a corner is cut off from the ground. That
     corner falls.
  4. Pick up a small fallen chunk, carry it, throw it, and drop it in a crate.
     Then smash a fallen chunk on the floor and watch it split.

  Two things I'd watch for:
  - A freshly broken piece starts flush against what's left, so it may sit there
    for a moment before falling.
  - A very large piece, like the whole top of the wall, will hit hard.

  Tell me if either feels off. Otherwise I'll move on to phase 3: the trowel and
  cementing debris back in.
❯ Do it

      179 +## Adds a voxel (construction, repair, cementing). Anchors onl
          +y apply to fixed                                              
      180 +## structures, where they mark voxels resting on the world.   
      181 +func add_voxel(cell: Vector3i, material: VoxelMaterialDefiniti
          +on, anchored: bool = false, cell_health: float = -1.0) -> void
          +:                                                             
      182 +  if cells.has(cell) or material == null:                     
      183 +    return                                                    
      184 +  var index := palette.find(material)                         
      185 +  if index < 0:                                               
      186 +    palette.append(material)                                  
      187 +    index = palette.size() - 1                                
      188 +  _set_cell(cell, index, cell_health)                         
      189 +  if anchored and freeze:                                     
      190 +    anchors[cell] = true                                      
      191 +                                                              
      192 +                                                              
      193 +## Removes a voxel and returns [material, health], e.g. to mov
          +e it into another body.                                       
      194 +func take_voxel(cell: Vector3i) -> Array:                     
      195 +  if not cells.has(cell):                                     
      196 +    return []                                                 
      197 +  var material := palette[cells[cell]]                        
      198 +  var cell_health: float = health.get(cell, material.strength)
      199 +  _remove_cell(cell)                                          
      200 +  return [material, cell_health]                              
      201 +                                                              
      202 +

          +ize: float, material: VoxelMaterialDefinition, anchored: bool)
          + -> VoxelBody:

          +ell) * size)

  - Using it: pick it up and equip it like any other tool (E, then F). A
    see-through cube follows the crosshair: green means it will fit, red means
    it's blocked by you or a loose object. Click to place one brick voxel, or
    hold to keep placing about three per second.
  - Aiming at a voxel face places the new voxel against that face. That's how
    you fill holes or extend a wall, pillar or broken piece.
  - Aiming at the floor or a fixed wall starts a new structure on a
    world-aligned grid. If you aim at the floor right next to something you've
    built, it extends that structure instead of starting a separate one.
  - Anchoring: voxels placed directly on the world count as foundations, so
    phase-2 collapse works on what you build. A wall you build and then knock
    the legs out from under falls like the authored ones. Repairing the bottom
    row of the original wall also restores its foundation.
  - The trowel is configured entirely in props/trowel.tres: what it places, the
    voxel size for new structures, its reach and its repeat speed. It's the
    existing tool system with a new Place Voxel action.

  Cementing debris back in:
  - Pick up a broken chunk, a rubble piece or one of the brick chunks, and push
    it against a structure. When it's in position, the prompt shows [C] Cement.
  - Pressing C snaps the chunk to the nearest 90° alignment, so you don't need
    to line it up perfectly. Its voxels then move into the structure, keeping
    their material and any damage.
  - Only voxels that land in empty cells connected to the structure go in. Any
    that don't fit stay in your hand as a smaller chunk, so material is never
    duplicated or lost.

  Try the full loop:
  1. Smash a hole through the wall.
  2. Pick up the rubble and the chunks that fell.
  3. Fit each piece into the hole and press C.
  4. Fill any gaps that are left with the trowel.

  Also try building a short wall on the floor, then knocking out its bottom row
  to watch it drop. R resets everything: built structures and broken pieces are
  cleared, and the authored ones are rebuilt.

  Known limits:
  - C only shows when a piece's voxels would land right next to the structure,
    within about one voxel, so press pieces in close.
  - Cemented voxels keep their own material, so concrete rubble pressed into the
    brick wall stays concrete.
  - The trowel always places brick. You can change this with Place Material in
    the inspector.

  The temporary _tmp_test/ folder from earlier is still in the project if you
  want it gone.

❯ Here's a question. Can you create Vox files for Magicavoxel?                  

● Yes. .vox is a simple, publicly documented binary format: a VOX  header, then
  chunks for size (SIZE), voxel positions with palette indices (XYZI) and
  colours (RGBA). I can write those bytes directly. There's no Python on this
  machine, but a GDScript tool run through Godot headless works fine.

  Here's how it could fit your project:

  - Export to MagicaVoxel: save any VoxelBody or structure definition (the wall,
    a pillar, something you built with the trowel) as a .vox you can open and
    edit in MagicaVoxel. Materials map to palette colours.
  - Import from MagicaVoxel: the more useful direction. A .vox →
    VoxelStructureDefinition importer would let you model walls, furniture or
    Taskmaster props in MagicaVoxel and drop them in as destructible,
    collapsible, rebuildable objects. Each palette colour would map to one of
    your voxel materials (brick, concrete, wood and so on) through a small
    mapping resource. The installed add-on already has a .vox parser
    (vox_importer.gd) I can borrow from, but it feeds the add-on's own format,
    so the glue would be ours.
  - Generate models: I can also write .vox files from a description ("a 20×10×20
    brick hut with a doorway") for you to refine in MagicaVoxel.

  A few limits:
  - A single .vox model is capped at 256 voxels per axis. Bigger builds need
    several models in MagicaVoxel's scene graph, which I can handle.
  - The palette holds at most 255 colours.
  - I can't open MagicaVoxel myself. I'd check files by parsing them back, but
    you'd be the one confirming they look right.

  Which do you want: the importer, an exporter, a few generated models, or a
  combination?

❯ Can you make a simple table in Magic of Voxel, or as a Vox model, and then

  a destructible item.

  edge, and four 1×1 legs, which should make for satisfying breaks.

● The table is ready, saved in the project at props/vox/table.vox, and attached
  above.

  What's in it: 148 voxels, 12 wide × 8 deep × 8 tall, which comes out at 1.2 ×
  0.8 × 0.8 m with our 0.1 m voxels.
  - A planked top in two light wood shades.
  - A darker wooden frame under the edge of the top.
  - Four 1×1 legs, so knocking out legs should make it tip or collapse nicely.

  I read the file back and its structure checks out: correct header, all 148
  voxels present. I can't open MagicaVoxel myself, so please open it there to
  check it looks right, and tweak it if you like. MagicaVoxel saves in the same
  format.

  One thing to know before you import it: the VoxelDestruction add-on registers
  its own .vox importer, so Godot will turn the file into the add-on's voxel
  format, not our destructible system. Because the file is inside the project
  folder, your editor will probably do that automatically on its next scan.
  That's harmless, but the result won't break, collapse or rebuild with our
  tools yet.

  To make it a destructible item in our system, the next step is a small
  converter. It reads the .vox and produces a VoxelStructureDefinition our
  VoxelBody can use. Each palette colour maps to one of our voxel materials; for
  this table that would be a new wood material, weaker than brick and lighter,
  so legs snap off easily. The table would then be a free physics object you can
  smash, collapse, pick up the pieces of and cement back together. Want me to
  build that?

  How the import works: structure definitions now have a MagicaVoxel section.

  The table (props/voxel_structures/table.tres):
  - It uses a new wood material (props/voxel_materials/wood.tres). Wood is
    weaker than brick: a half-charged hammer swing takes a chunk out, and a full
    one snaps a leg. About 30% of knocked-out voxels become rubble.
  - Tables aren't anchored, so they're free physics objects. You can push one by
    walking into it, pick it up, throw it, or knock its legs out so it tips.
  - They weigh about 27 kg. I set wood's density low on purpose: the voxels are
    solid 10 cm cubes, so real wood density would make the table about 96 kg and
    too heavy to carry. Raise it in wood.tres if you'd rather tables were
    immovable.

❯ a few things. If, like, you wiggle the mouse button, or wiggle the mouse and  
  then hit the throw button, it should throw the object, like it should launch  
  the object as well. And also, can you make it so objects can be broken by     
  throwing them? So like if I throw the table at a wall, it can shatter both    
  the table and the wall for instance                                           

● Two changes:

  1. Flick throws. Right now a throw replaces the object's velocity, so any
     swing you gave it with the mouse is thrown away. I'll add that swing on top
     of the throw, so whipping the mouse sideways or upward as you release
     launches the object that way. It's capped and set in the inspector. Mass
     still matters, because a heavy object can't swing as fast to begin with.
  2. Impact breaking. Thrown or falling objects damage voxels when they hit
     hard. Each moving object measures its own sudden change in velocity when it
     touches something new; that measures how hard it hit. It damages itself if
     it's a voxel object (the table) and whatever it hit if that's a fixed
     voxel structure (the wall). Gentle contacts like stacking, resting or
     sliding stay below a speed threshold and do nothing.

  First the flick:

● Both changes are in and the playground loads without errors. I haven't
  play-tested either.

  Flick throws: when you throw, the object now keeps the swing you're giving it
  with the mouse. Whip the mouse sideways, upward or forward as you release and
  it launches that way, on top of the normal throw. Charged throws work the same
  way: hold LMB, wind up the swing, release.
  - On the player's ObjectManipulator node: Flick Influence (1.0; 0 turns it
    off, 2 doubles it) and Max Flick Speed (12 m/s).
  - Heavy objects can't swing as fast while held, so flicking the metal box does
    far less than flicking a cardboard box.

  Breaking things by throwing them:
  - A hard crash damages voxels where things hit.
    - A voxel object like the table breaks itself on impact. Throw it at the
      concrete throwing wall and it shatters.
    - A voxel wall or structure gets damaged by whatever hits it. Throw the
      table at the brick wall and both take damage. The table (wood) breaks more
      than the wall (brick).
    - Ordinary props break voxel walls too. A thrown crate cracks brick, and the
      heavy metal box hits twice as hard.
    - Gentle contact does nothing: stacking, setting things on a voxel table,
      sliding, and resting all stay below the impact threshold.
  - Damage scales with how hard the hit is (mass × change in speed), so a soft
    lob only scuffs.
  - Pieces that break off keep their speed, and everything else carries on as
    before: collapse, rubble you can pick up, cementing back in.

  Tuning:
  - Per object: a new Impact Damage Scale setting in each object's .tres. Metal
    box 2.0, cardboard 0.3, rubber ball 0, everything else 1.0. Your purple
    "Ball" is left at the default, but at 0.5 kg it's too light to break
    anything anyway.
  - Global: the threshold and strength are constants at the top of
    physics/voxels/impact_breaker.gd. An impact needs a velocity change of at
    least 3.5 m/s, and damage is 0.15 per N·s above that.

  Watch for: a voxel table thrown at the ground or knocked off something high
  can also chip itself on the floor, because a hard landing counts as an impact.
  If it breaks too easily, raise Wood's strength in wood.tres or the threshold
  in impact_breaker.gd.

❯ I guess the next evolution would almost be like if you're holding stuff and   
  you swing it at something that's destructible. So say, for example, again, if 
  you're just holding the table and you swing it at the wall, both of them can  
  break. Or if you're holding the bat or the hammer or, you know, the golf      
  club or anything like that, and you swing it with enough force, not by        
  holding down the mouse button, just by physically moving your mouse, stuff    
  like that.                                                                    

● Two parts:

  1. Held objects as weapons: the impact breaking from last time already works
     for carried objects. I'd switched it off while an object is held, to avoid
     false hits from the carry steering. The steering can only change an
     object's speed by about 1.3 m/s per physics step, well under the 3.5 m/s
     impact threshold, so it's safe to switch on. Swing a held table into the
     wall and both break; bash the wall with a held crate and the wall takes
     damage.
  2. Mouse-swung tools: while a tool is equipped and idle, I'll track how fast
     its striking point (the hammer head, bat barrel or club head) is moving,
     ignoring your own walking speed. Above a threshold, it sweeps along the
     head's actual path and hits whatever it passes through. Power comes from
     how fast you swung, through the same impact path as LMB swings. So a hard
     flick breaks voxels and launches balls, and a lazy wave does nothing. Golf
     works naturally: the club ignores looking up and down, so a sideways mouse
     sweep swings it along the ground.

  First, let held objects count:

● Now call it each tick, and reset tracking when a tool is equipped, so the

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Both are in and the playground loads without errors. I haven't play-tested
  this.

  Bashing with held objects: carried objects now count for impact breaking.
  Swing a held table into the brick wall with the mouse and both take damage.
  Bash the wall with a held crate or the metal box and the wall cracks; the
  metal box hits twice as hard. Grinding an object slowly along a wall or
  pressing it against one does nothing. It takes a real swing: an impact of at
  least 3.5 m/s.

  Swinging equipped tools with the mouse: with a tool equipped and no button
  held, flick the mouse and the tool's head moves with the camera. The game
  tracks how fast the head is moving, minus your own walking speed. Fast enough,
  and it sweeps along the path the head actually travelled and hits what's
  there, through the same impact code as LMB swings (impulse, voxel damage,
  rubble, feedback).
  - Power comes from swing speed. Below 5 m/s nothing happens. Between 5 and 14
    m/s it scales from the tool's tap power up to full power, so a hard flick of
    the hammer punches a hole and a lazy wave doesn't.
  - The golf club ignores looking up and down, so a fast sideways mouse sweep
    swings it low along the ground. You can putt or drive a ball without
    clicking.
  - One hit per 0.25 s, and nothing triggers mid-LMB-swing, while equipping, or
    for the trowel.
  - Per tool, a new Motion Strikes section in each tool's .tres: on/off, Min
    Speed (5), Full Speed (14) and Cooldown (0.25 s).

  Unlike LMB swings, mouse swings hit with the actual tool head, not the
  crosshair. That's deliberate, because it's a physical swing, so you'll need to
  be within arm's reach.

  Mouse sensitivity matters here. If flicks trigger too easily or not at all,
  adjust Min Speed and Full Speed per tool. Raise them if walking past things
  with a tool out causes hits, or lower them if you have to flick wildly to
  connect.

❯ Okay. Can you now remake everything, all of the existing assets in Vox models 
  and import them and all that sort of stuff for things like the tools and the  
  balls and the crates and all that sort of stuff? Any of the stuff you can     
  hold physically in your hand, don't let them be deformed.                     

● I'll make sure I've got this right. You want every holdable object (tools,
  balls, crates, boxes, containers, blocks, the cylinder) to look like
  MagicaVoxel models. They should keep behaving exactly as now: same collision,
  physics, grabbing, containers and tool use, and not become destructible.

  My approach:

  1. A generator (tools/generate_prop_vox.gd) that voxelises each object
     straight from its current definition. It uses the real shape parts,
     materials and detail meshes (crate frame, tape, bands, stripes), with
     subtle shade variation and plank lines on wooden things. It writes one .vox
     per object into props/vox/ that you can open and repaint in MagicaVoxel.
  2. A "Vox Model" visual option on object definitions. When set, the object is
     drawn from the .vox file instead of primitive meshes, but keeps its
     existing collision shapes. Nothing deforms or breaks, and metal stays
     metallic because the object's own material settings carry over.
  3. Run the generator once and point all 14 holdable objects at their new
     models.

  First, writing .vox files and building static meshes from them:

      132 +## A static, face-culled, vertex-coloured mesh of a model, its
          + first cell's corner                                          
      133 +## at `origin`. Cached per file/size/origin/material.         
      134 +static func build_mesh(path: String, voxel_size: float, origin
          +: Vector3, material: Material) -> ArrayMesh:                  
      135 +  var key := "%s|%s|%s|%d" % [path, voxel_size, origin, materi
          +al.get_instance_id() if material else 0]                      
      136 +  if _mesh_cache.has(key):                                    
      137 +    return _mesh_cache[key]                                   
      138 +  var data := read_cells(path)                                
      139 +  if data.is_empty():                                         
      140 +    return null                                               
      141 +  var cells: Dictionary = data["cells"]                       
      142 +  var tool := SurfaceTool.new()                               
      143 +  tool.begin(Mesh.PRIMITIVE_TRIANGLES)                        
      144 +  for cell: Vector3i in cells:                                
      145 +    var color: Color = cells[cell]                            
      146 +    for face in VoxelMesher.FACES:                            
      147 +      if cells.has(cell + face[0]):                           
      148 +        continue                                              
      149 +      var corners: Array = face[1]                            
      150 +      for index in [0, 1, 2, 0, 2, 3]:                        
      151 +        tool.set_color(color)                                 
      152 +        tool.set_normal(Vector3(face[0]))                     
      153 +        tool.add_vertex(origin + (Vector3(cell) + corners[inde
          +x]) * voxel_size)                                             
      154 +  tool.set_material(material if material != null else VoxelMes
          +her.get_material())                                           
      155 +  var mesh := tool.commit()                                   
      156 +  _mesh_cache[key] = mesh                                     
      157 +  return mesh

  └───────────────────────────┴─────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

  Each model has slight shade variation so surfaces aren't flat.

  How they're wired in:
  - Object definitions have a new Vox Model setting (file, voxel size, origin)
    in the Visuals section. When it's set, the object is drawn from the .vox
    file instead of its primitive meshes and detail scene.
  - Collision, mass, physics, containers and tool behaviour are untouched. The
    model is purely visual, so nothing deforms or breaks.
  - Each object's own material settings carry over, so the metal box stays shiny
    metal and the basket stays glossy plastic. The colours come from the model.

  Editing in MagicaVoxel: open any file in props/vox/, repaint or reshape it,
  and save; the change shows up in Godot. If you change a model's dimensions,
  adjust Vox Voxel Size and Vox Origin on the object so it still lines up with
  its collision.

  Regenerating: tools/generate_prop_vox.gd rebuilds the models from the object
  definitions. Run it only if you change an object's shapes, because it
  overwrites the .vox files and any hand edits with them. To protect hand-edited
  models, remove them from the list at the top of the script. Command:

  "C:/Users/evans/Desktop/Godot_v4.7.1-stable_win64.exe" --headless --path
  "E:/Projects/tm-game" -s res://tools/generate_prop_vox.gd

  Housekeeping:
  - The script re-saved the object .tres files, and saving outside the editor
    dropped their internal IDs. I put the original IDs back, and the script now
    keeps them itself. If your editor asks to reload resources from disk, say
    yes.
  - The old detail scenes in props/details/ are no longer used by these objects,
    but I've left them in place. If you clear an object's Vox Model, it goes
    back to the old look.

❯ I did some tinkering with the graphics settings before, and all the voxel     
  models look like this. Can you keep the graphics settings I put earlier, but  
  fix the lighting and all that sort of stuff around the voxel models?          

● Your screenshot shows the problem clearly: the lighting on every voxel surface
  is inside out. The table top faces the sun but renders dark, while the sides
  and edges render bright. The ball's banding and the crate's flat look are the
  same issue.

  The cause is in my code, not your graphics settings. I wound the triangles of
  each voxel face in the wrong order. Godot treats clockwise triangles as the
  front, and mine were counter-clockwise. I'd also turned off back-face culling,
  which hid the mistake: Godot drew the "back" of every face and flipped its
  normal for lighting, so lit faces went dark and shaded ones went bright. Your
  settings just made it more obvious.

  Let me check what you changed, so I know I'm not touching it:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Those rendering changes explain why inside-out faces looked wrong, since those
  effects rely on surface normals. Now I'll fix it by reversing the triangle
  order in both voxel mesh builders and re-enabling back-face culling.
