● I've fixed the lighting on all voxel models, and your graphics settings are
  untouched.

  What was wrong: my voxel mesh code built every face back to front. I'd also
  switched off back-face culling, which hid the mistake: Godot was drawing the
  back of each face and lighting it as if it pointed inward. Surfaces facing the
  sun went dark and shaded ones went bright. SSAO, SSIL and SDFGI all read
  those same surface directions, so they made it look worse.

  The fix:
  - Every voxel face now has the correct front side. I checked all six face
    directions to confirm none would disappear.
  - Back-face culling is back on. That's correct now, and slightly cheaper to
    draw.
  - One shared setting drives both voxel mesh builders, so this applies to the
    prop models (crates, balls, tools, containers), the voxel tables, the brick
    wall, pillar and bridge, plus built structures, rubble and broken pieces.

  Left alone: the Forward+ renderer you switched to, and SSAO, SSIL, SDFGI, glow
  and tonemapping in the playground environment.

  Run the game and tops of objects should catch the sun, with ambient occlusion
  in the corners of boxes and containers. The playground loads without errors,
  but I couldn't render a frame headless to check it. If anything still looks
  off, send another screenshot. If a model looks hollow or has holes, tell me
  which one.

❯ Godot Physics Sandbox — Cartoon Physics and Impact Feel Pass                  
  Improve the existing Godot 4 physics sandbox to make its physics,             
  movement-driven interactions and destruction more playful, responsive and     
  slightly exaggerated.                                                         
  This is a targeted physics tuning and polish pass, not a redesign or systems  
  rewrite.                                                                      
  The intended feel is approximately 70% believable physics and 30% cartoon     
  exaggeration. Think satisfying impacts, energetic tumbles, powerful throws    
  and entertaining destruction, without turning everything into weightless      
  slapstick.                                                                    
  1. Preserve existing functionality                                            
  Inspect the current project before editing.                                   
  Preserve all existing systems, including:                                     
                                                                                
  * First-person movement and camera controls.                                  
  * Physics-based grabbing, carrying, rotating, dropping and throwing.          
  * Mouse-flick throws and chargeable throws.                                   
  * Physically swinging held objects into other objects.                        
  * Equippable tools and mouse-driven motion strikes.                           
  * Chargeable tool strikes and crosshair-based targeting.                      
  * Physical containers and their contents.                                     
  * Data-driven `.tres` physics object definitions.                             
  * Voxel destruction, structural collapse and debris.                          
  * Destructible MagicaVoxel objects and their import pipeline.                 
  * Construction, trowel placement and cementing.                               
  * Existing playground objects, scenes and editor changes.                     
                                                                                
  Do not replace Godot's physics with scripted or canned animations.            
  2. More satisfying object manipulation                                        
  Make ordinary physical objects slightly more responsive and enjoyable to      
  handle.                                                                       
                                                                                
  * Reduce excessive sluggishness when moving and swinging reasonably light     
  objects.                                                                      
  * Preserve mass-dependent behaviour: heavy objects must still feel heavy.     
  * Improve responsiveness when rapidly changing the direction of a held        
  object.                                                                       
  * Make sideways swings and overhead movements feel powerful without allowing  
  uncontrolled acceleration.                                                    
  * Preserve proper collision handling while objects are held.                  
  * Avoid excessive shaking, instability, clipping or unrealistic               
  teleportation.                                                                
                                                                                
  The player should enjoy physically swinging a chair, crate, hammer or table   
  around.                                                                       
  Do not simply multiply every object's velocity indiscriminately.              
  3. Exaggerated impacts                                                        
  Make strong collisions feel more energetic.                                   
  When a fast-moving object strikes another object or a destructible surface:   
                                                                                
  * Apply a slightly exaggerated but controlled impulse.                        
  * Give struck objects satisfying knockback.                                   
  * Introduce appropriate angular impulse so objects can tumble, spin and flip. 
  * Allow powerful impacts to create entertaining physical chain reactions.     
  * Preserve sensible behaviour for gentle contact, stacking, resting and       
  sliding.                                                                      
  * Respect mass and material differences.                                      
                                                                                
  For example:                                                                  
                                                                                
  * A wooden box struck by a hammer might tumble dramatically.                  
  * A rubber ball hit with a golf club should launch with satisfying speed.     
  * A chair thrown into a wall should recoil, rotate and potentially break.     
  * A heavy metal box should deliver a powerful impact without behaving like a  
  lightweight cardboard box.                                                    
                                                                                
  Keep the exaggerated response deterministic and consistent wherever           
  practical.                                                                    
  4. More entertaining destruction                                              
  Improve the physical presentation and feel of destruction using the existing  
  voxel and impact systems.                                                     
                                                                                
  * Make powerful impacts produce more energetic debris.                        
  * Add some controlled outward scattering and rotation to detached fragments.  
  * Give broken-off chunks convincing momentum inherited from the impact.       
  * Make collisions between detached chunks and other objects feel satisfying.  
  * Allow strong collisions to produce natural secondary damage.                
  * Preserve structural connectivity and support calculations.                  
  * Keep destruction strength dependent on the existing material properties.    
                                                                                
  Avoid excessive explosions of debris from small impacts.                      
  A wooden table smashing into a brick wall should feel dramatic, but it should 
  not destroy an entire building unless the actual damage and structural        
  support justify it.                                                           
  Preserve the existing rubble limits and performance safeguards.               
  5. Improve throwing and physical swinging                                     
  Polish the systems already implemented.                                       
  Throws:                                                                       
                                                                                
  * Preserve hold-to-charge and release-to-throw.                               
  * Preserve mouse-flick momentum.                                              
  * Allow a little more energetic movement at high charge.                      
  * Keep control over maximum speed and impulse.                                
  * Preserve directional consistency and differences between light and heavy    
  objects.                                                                      
                                                                                
  Held-object swings:                                                           
                                                                                
  * Make deliberate mouse swings feel impactful.                                
  * Preserve collision-based damage to both the held object and its target.     
  * Avoid accidental damage from ordinary repositioning.                        
  * Consider angular velocity at the contact point, not merely centre-of-mass   
  velocity.                                                                     
                                                                                
  Equipped tools:                                                               
                                                                                
  * Preserve both chargeable LMB attacks and physical mouse-driven strikes.     
  * Make strong strikes feel punchier.                                          
  * Keep motion thresholds configurable to avoid accidental attacks.            
  * Preserve the different behaviours of the hammer, bat and golf club.         
                                                                                
  6. Impact feedback                                                            
  Add or refine lightweight audiovisual feedback to make interactions more      
  satisfying.                                                                   
  Consider:                                                                     
                                                                                
  * Brief, subtle camera shake for particularly powerful impacts.               
  * More expressive impact particles.                                           
  * Different impact sounds for wood, metal, stone and other materials where    
  practical.                                                                    
  * Stronger feedback for particularly forceful collisions.                     
  * A small amount of extra visual energy when a structure breaks apart.        
                                                                                
  Camera shake must be restrained, configurable and preferably disabled for     
  very small impacts.                                                           
  Do not introduce expensive post-processing effects or excessive screen        
  effects.                                                                      
  7. Data-driven configuration                                                  
  Make exaggeration tunable rather than hardcoding it throughout the physics    
  systems.                                                                      
  Prefer a reusable physics-feel configuration Resource, with sensible          
  properties such as:                                                           
                                                                                
  * Impact impulse multiplier.                                                  
  * Angular impulse multiplier.                                                 
  * Held-object responsiveness multiplier.                                      
  * Throw exaggeration multiplier.                                              
  * Debris launch multiplier.                                                   
  * Camera shake strength.                                                      
  * Minimum impact strength required for exaggerated feedback.                  
                                                                                
  Use this as a shared physics-feel profile where appropriate.                  
  Keep object mass, friction, bounce, strength and other material-specific      
  properties in their existing Resources.                                       
  Allow different games using this framework to have different physics-feel     
  profiles in future.                                                           
  For example:                                                                  
                                                                                
  * Grounded — mostly realistic responses.                                      
  * Playful — slightly exaggerated, the intended default for this game.         
  * Chaotic — stronger exaggeration for experimentation.                        
                                                                                
  These should be configuration presets, not separate physics implementations.  
  Avoid unnecessarily complicating the architecture or exposing dozens of       
  redundant settings.                                                           
  8. Stability and performance                                                  
  The game currently performs well, including while destroying voxel            
  structures. Preserve that.                                                    
                                                                                
  * Avoid unnecessary per-frame allocations and expensive physics queries.      
  * Avoid unbounded feedback effects, debris and collision chains.              
  * Do not multiply impulses repeatedly for one sustained collision.            
  * Prevent accidental feedback loops where two objects continually amplify     
  each other's velocities.                                                      
  * Do not make objects unstable at rest.                                       
  * Preserve the existing voxel dirty-chunk and debris-cap optimisations.       
                                                                                
  9. Scope and verification                                                     
  This is a polish pass on working systems.                                     
  Make targeted improvements rather than replacing the physics framework.       
  Do not add new weapons, props, gameplay modes, levels or unrelated features.  
  Preserve any changes I've made in the Godot editor while you're working.      
  As previously requested, do not spend time writing extensive automated        
  gameplay tests. Perform a basic Godot load/parse check to catch errors, then  
  let me handle gameplay testing.                                               
  When finished, explain the settings you've added, where to find them in the   
  Inspector, and what gameplay differences I should notice.                     
  Desired outcome                                                               
  The entire sandbox should feel slightly more cartoonish, energetic and        
  satisfying without losing believable physics.                                 
  A ball should fly satisfyingly when struck. A crate should tumble             
  entertainingly. A table smashed into a wall should produce dramatic but       
  physically meaningful destruction.                                            
  The guiding principle is believable physics with exaggerated consequences —   
  not random chaos.                                                             
  This project is also intended to become a reusable framework for future Godot 
  3D games, so keep the feel configurable and independent from any specific     
  game's rules.                                                                 

● This is a tuning pass, so I'll keep it targeted: one shared feel profile that
  plugs into the code paths that already exist. Here's the plan:

  Area: Feel profile
  Change: New PhysicsFeel resource with 7 settings, plus presets Grounded /
  Playful (default) / Chaotic. The playground picks the profile in its
  inspector.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Holding
  Change: Stiffer, quicker carry steering for light objects. The force limit is
  untouched, so heavy stays heavy.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Throws
  Change: A bit more speed at high charge, plus an end-over-end tumble that
  scales with charge and lightness.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Impacts
  Change: Tool hits and hard collisions get a bigger impulse and a deterministic

  tumble spin, scaled by mass (light things flip, heavy things barely rock).
  Collision impact is now measured at the contact point including spin, so a
  swung table's end counts.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Destruction
  Change: Rubble speed scales with hit strength. Broken-off chunks inherit the
  impact's direction with some outward scatter and spin, weaker the further
  they are from the hit. Big breaks get a dust burst.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Feedback
  Change: Material-based synthesised sounds (wood, metal, stone, plastic,
  rubber,
  cardboard), stronger particles for strong hits, and restrained
  distance-limited camera shake. A cap on live effects keeps chain reactions
  bounded.

  Let me look at the current state of the files I'll touch, since you may have
  edited things:

● The feel pass is done. The playground and your original main scene both load
  without errors. As agreed, I haven't play-tested anything.

  The new setting: one Physics Feel profile

  Where to find it: select the playground's root node (PhysicsPlayground) and
  look for Physics Feel in the Inspector. It's set to playful.tres. Swap it for
  grounded.tres or chaotic.tres from res://physics/feel/, or click into it to
  tune the values live.

  Setting: Impact Impulse Multiplier
  Grounded: 1.0
  Playful: 1.25
  Chaotic: 1.7
  What it does: Extra push from strong tool hits and crashes. The launch-speed
  cap scales with it.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Setting: Angular Impulse Multiplier
  Grounded: 0
  Playful: 1.0
  Chaotic: 2.0
  What it does: Tumble and flip spin on impacts and charged throws.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Setting: Held Responsiveness
  Grounded: 1.0
  Playful: 1.3
  Chaotic: 1.6
  What it does: How snappily held objects follow and turn.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Setting: Throw Multiplier
  Grounded: 1.0
  Playful: 1.15
  Chaotic: 1.4
  What it does: Extra speed at full charge. Tap throws are unchanged.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Setting: Debris Launch Multiplier
  Grounded: 1.0
  Playful: 1.4
  Chaotic: 2.2
  What it does: How hard rubble and broken chunks fly.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Setting: Camera Shake Strength
  Grounded: 0.4
  Playful: 1.0
  Chaotic: 1.6
  What it does: Shake from big nearby impacts. 0 turns it off.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Setting: Min Exaggeration Impulse
  Grounded: 10
  Playful: 6 N·s
  Chaotic: 4
  What it does: Impacts below this get no extras at all.

  Material-specific values (mass, friction, bounce, strength) stay in each
  object's own .tres. The one new per-object setting is Impact Sound, in each
  object definition and voxel material, set to Wood, Metal, Stone, Plastic,
  Rubber or Cardboard.

  What you should notice

  - Holding: light things (crates, tools, the table) follow and turn noticeably
    snappier, and quick direction changes feel less floaty. The metal box still
    lags, because the carry force limit wasn't touched.
  - Throws: full-charge throws go about 15% further and tumble end over end.
    Light objects spin more and heavy ones barely rotate. Taps and mouse flicks
    behave as before.
  - Tool hits: strong hits push 25% harder and add a tumble based on mass. A
    crate hit with the hammer flips; the metal box just rocks. The golf ball
    leaves at about 27 m/s instead of 22. Strong hits throw more and bigger
    particles and give a small camera shake.
  - Crashes:
    - Impact speed is now measured at the contact point, including spin, so the
      far end of a swung table counts even when its middle is barely moving.
    - On a strong impact, the crashing object recoils with a spin.
    - A loose object it hits gets a little extra knockback and a tumble, about
      half of the extra push and always under 1×, so two objects can't keep
      feeding each other.
    - Every strong impact now makes its material's sound, even when nothing
      breaks.
  - Destruction: pieces that break off within half a second of a hit fly along
    the hit's direction, with some outward scatter and spin. They're strongest
    near the blow and weaker for big or distant pieces, so a pillar cut at its
    base still mostly falls rather than exploding. Rubble speed scales with hit
    strength, so taps produce gentle crumbs and full swings spray. Sections of 8
    or more voxels breaking away give a dust puff, and big ones give a short
    thud and shake. Damage amounts, structural support and the rubble cap are
    all unchanged.
  - Sound: synthesised material sounds. Wood knocks, metal rings, stone is
    gritty, plastic clacks, cardboard thuds. Bigger hits are louder and slightly
    deeper.

  Safeguards

  - Every extra applies once per impact, using the existing 150 ms per-object
    cooldown, so sustained contact never repeats them.
  - Gentle contact gets nothing extra: resting, stacking, sliding and anything
    under the threshold behave exactly as before.
  - At most 16 impact effects exist at once, so big chain reactions stay cheap.
  - Camera shake is distance-limited (10 m) and squared, so small hits are
    invisible.
  - The voxel chunk-rebuild batching and the rubble cap are untouched.

  The ball props now also report contacts, purely so they make sounds when they
  hit things.

❯ I would like you to make like things like the bat and hammer and golf club    
  and all that sort of stuff better to use at the moment. For example, like the 
  bat is like, even when equipped, it's halfway off the screen, so you can't    
  swing it at anything. And the hammer is too small. Can you replace that       
  model, like the voxel model, with a sledgehammer instead? And just in         
  general, just improve the tools.                                              

● Here's the plan for the tools:

  1. Bat: its resting pose leans it back over your right shoulder, so most of it
     is off-screen. I'll bring it forward into view, angled up and in toward
     the centre, with a wind-up that stays partly visible while you charge.
  2. Hammer → sledgehammer: a new long-handled, heavy-headed tool in the same
     slot, so scenes that reference the hammer keep working. It has a two-handed
     resting pose with the head visible, an overhead wind-up and a downward
     smash. It's heavier and slower, but hits much harder with a bigger voxel
     crater. I'll regenerate its voxel model from the new shape.
  3. All tools: a little sway, so equipped tools trail slightly behind the
     camera when you turn instead of being rigidly glued to it, which makes them
     feel held. It's capped so it never drifts far, and you can tune or disable
     it on the player's ToolHandler.

      357 +func _update_sway(delta: float) -> void:

          +es <= 0.0:

      365 +  var limit := deg_to_rad(max_sway_degrees)

  Bat: it now rests in front of you, angled up into the upper-right of the

  your shoulder. Charging cocks it to the right with the handle still visible.

  screen, mouse-swung strikes can actually connect.

  Hammer → sledgehammer:
  - It's in the same spot on the workbench, keeping the same file, so everything
    that referenced the hammer still works.
  - New voxel model: a 0.85 m wooden handle with a dark rubber grip, and a heavy
    steel head with brighter striking faces.
  - Holding it: two-handed, with the head resting in the upper right of your
    view. Charging lifts it overhead, and releasing brings it down in an
    overhead smash.
  - Feel: 4.5 kg, slower swing, 0.9 s to full charge.

  The sledgehammer won't fire mouse-swing strikes accidentally as easily as the
  hammer did.

  All tools: equipped tools now sway slightly, trailing behind the camera when
  you turn and catching up smoothly. It's capped at 8°, so a tool never drifts
  out of place. This applies to the golf club, trowel, sledgehammer and bat.
  Tune it on the player's ToolHandler node: Sway Response (16; higher is
  tighter, 0 turns it off) and Max Sway Degrees (8; 0 turns it off). I didn't
  change the golf club's hold.

  Model generator: you can now rebuild just named models, so models you've
  edited in MagicaVoxel are left alone. I used that for the sledgehammer:

  "C:/Users/evans/Desktop/Godot_v4.7.1-stable_win64.exe" --headless --path
  "E:/Projects/tm-game" -s res://tools/generate_prop_vox.gd -- hammer

  If a pose looks off: each tool's .tres has a Poses section (Rest, Windup and
  Strike Position and Rotation Degrees), and you can edit it while the game
  runs. Rough guide:
  - Rest Position X moves the tool left or right on screen, Y moves it up or
    down, and Z sets its distance from you.
  - Rest Rotation X tilts it toward you or away, and Z leans it in or out.

● Here's what I'd build. Everything is made of voxels and plugs into the systems
  you already have: grabbing, throwing, containers, tools, impacts, destruction
  and cementing.

  New tools (equippable, F to equip)

  Each one handles differently through its tool settings, without any
  special-case code.

  New objects

  Throwable and holdable props (not destructible):
  - Balls: bowling ball (heavy), football, basketball, beach ball (very light
    and floaty), tennis balls, hockey puck
  - Rubber duck, traffic cone, car tyre (bouncy, rolls), oil barrel (rolls),
    anvil (extremely heavy)
  - Giant foam die, coffee mug, teapot, garden gnome, Taskmaster-style trophy
  - Single brick, wooden plank, pallet
  - Skittles set: 10 pins and a ball

  Destructible voxel objects (break, collapse, and can be cemented back
  together):
  - Wooden chair, stool, small bookshelf, crate stack
  - Watermelon, green outside and red inside, so smashing it shows the flesh
  - Pumpkin, same idea in orange
  - Glass pane, using a new fragile glass material that shatters into lots of
    small pieces
  - Snowman, brick chimney, small concrete bollard

  That's 10 tools and about 30 objects. There may be a few more or fewer once
  I'm building.

  Spawn menu

  - Q opens a menu with tabs: Tools / Props / Balls / Destructible / Containers.
    Click an item to spawn it in front of you, where the crosshair points.
  - Random button, plus G to spawn a random object at the crosshair instantly
    without opening the menu.
  - Clear spawned button removes everything you spawned. R still resets the
    original playground.
  - The menu fills itself by scanning props/ and props/voxel_structures/. Any
    new object or .tres you add later shows up automatically, with no list to
    maintain.
  - The mouse is freed while the menu is open. Q and G aren't bound to anything
    right now.

  How I'd build them

  - Tools and props: defined from shape parts the same way as the current tools,
    so they get proper collision. The model generator then makes their voxel
    art, and the art is editable in MagicaVoxel.
  - Destructible objects: generated straight as .vox models, including interior
    colours (melon flesh, pumpkin insides), and loaded through the MagicaVoxel
    structure pipeline.
  - Materials: a few new voxel materials (glass, melon, pumpkin, snow).
  - Playground: spawned items go into the scene at runtime. The playground scene
    itself stays untouched apart from one small hook for the menu.

  Want me to go ahead with all of it? Or tell me what to add, drop or change
  first, such as different keys or more of one category.

● Going ahead with all of it. Build order:

  1. Engine bits:
     - a PIERCE impact type and per-material damage multipliers (so axes chop
       wood and pickaxes chew stone)
     - a glass sound
     - multi-material MagicaVoxel models (for a wooden-framed glass pane)
     - an optional spawn category on definitions
  2. A content generator script that defines all the new tools, props, materials
     and destructibles in code, writes them as normal .tres files and .vox
     models, and runs the voxeliser. That's much less error-prone than
     hand-writing about 40 resource files, and you can still edit every result
     in the inspector or MagicaVoxel.
  3. The spawn menu, which builds itself at runtime with no playground scene
     edits.

  Starting with the engine bits:
